- [ ] **S8 debug info (built 2026-10-23), what is left.** (1) A local of a
      suspending function that lives across a suspension prints nothing once
      LLVM's coroutine split moves it into the frame (its `dbg.declare` is not
      carried over; `driver/debuginfo_test.go` keeps such locals in a plain
      function for that reason). (2) Lists, maps, sets, nullables, sealed
      values and closures are named and sized only; a gdb pretty-printer or
      DWARF members would show their contents. (3) std's files are embedded,
      so a debugger cannot show their text (writing them to a cache directory
      would). (4) lldb on macOS was not run. (5) `--release` builds carry no
      line information (`-g` without `!dbg`). (6) Function types in the
      subprograms are empty (`!DISubroutineType(types: !{})`).
- [ ] **D198 (built 2026-10-23, plan S16), what is left.** (1) A method
      called on a narrowed boxed variant gets a fresh box as `this`; if the
      method keeps that pointer (stores it somewhere), a later copy of the
      sealed value shares the box and a write through the kept pointer is seen
      by the copy. Every write through the value itself, `&value` included
      (it points at the tag and pointer, not the box), copies first; the D188
      escape summary would tell when a kept `this` needs a copy at assignment. (2) A box is copied on every write
      through a narrowed variable, not once per value (no uniqueness is
      tracked). (3) `bench/parse` was 1.1× Go on Windows (1.0–1.1× on Linux)
      before the instruction-count pass of 2026-10-23 (progress log), which
      found Veles executing ~40% fewer instructions than Go for the same work.
      The three choices that were left were decided 2026-10-24 as D200 (a
      `[runtime] gc` setting), D201 (a field moves out of a local whose methods
      were called) and D202 (`startsWith(prefix, startIndex)`); plan S18–S20.
      (4) macOS was not run.
- [ ] **D200–D202 (built 2026-10-24, plan S18–S20), what is left.** (1)
      D201's method summary is pessimistic where it cannot see: a method
      reached through a trait (a default body), a decorated or extern one, and
      a field mention it cannot classify (an index expression, a pointer taken
      with `&`) keep everything; an element written through `ref` in a method
      counts as handing out the storage. (2) `endsWith` has no index argument
      (D202 left it out until something needs it). (3) The Go front end's own
      lexer was not changed (its operator scan is `strings.HasPrefix` already).
      (4) macOS was not run.
- [ ] **C9 — `std/cli`, `std/build`, D203 (built 2026-10-24), what is
      left.** (1) **`examples/todo` keeps its own parser**: no command means
      `ls`, `list`/`do`/`del` are aliases, and `ls -passport` excludes a word —
      a default subcommand, variant aliases and pass-through arguments are
      `std/cli` API to decide first. (2) **`T.doc`** (a type's doc comment, a
      constant) was added for the help's first paragraph; the name is the
      agent's, not yet confirmed by the user. ~~(3) enum choices in the help~~,
      ~~(4) `has`/`attr` on `MemberInfo`~~, ~~(5) `internal` trait methods~~,
      ~~(6) inference through `try`~~, ~~(7) the `'enter'` message~~ — fixed
      2026-10-24 (D205's `Enumerated` lists the choices). (8) A reserved flag
      name (`--help`, `--version`, `-h`) or a long short flag is a compile-time
      `static assert` now, naming the field, but reported at the line in
      `std/cli` rather than at the user's field (a "required by" note at the
      use site would be a general improvement). (9) D160's per-OS files
      (`_windows.vs`, …) are plan P1, not built. (10) macOS was not run.
- [ ] **D207 (built 2026-10-24, plan C11), what is left.** (1) A block
      passed to a function of one's own (`buildString { … }`) is a closure, not
      inlined: there is no `inline fun`, so captures cost an allocation there.
      (2) `return` in a block given to `apply`/`edit` is refused; Kotlin returns
      from the enclosing function — a decision if wanted. (3) A receiver
      function value is called `body(x)`; `x.body()` is not offered (a
      spelling decision). (4) Inside `edit` on a string the receiver is a
      `StringBuilder`: it has the in-place edits and `len`/`isEmpty`, not yet
      the read-only string methods (`contains`, `startsWith`, …). (5) In a
      generic function, a block's bare names are resolved against the receiver
      type of the first instance checked. (6) `edit` on a struct copies it
      shallowly, as `copy()` does: a mutable collection in a field is shared
      with the original. (7) Inside an argument list that already failed, a
      block is not checked (its errors appear once the call is fixed). (8) A
      method calling `this.apply { … }` makes its callers' receivers escape
      (heap) whatever the block does — the receiver pass sees a pointer into
      `this` handed out. (9) Not measured on Linux.
- [x] **Fixed 2026-10-31 (plan N1c): intermittent on Linux (found
      2026-10-24), `driver TestExecutors` panicked "deadlock: every task is
      blocked"** in case 12 (a task waiting for a `Thread`) or 13
      (`runBlocking`). Cause: `os_thread_main` published `status = 1`, which
      lets `Thread.start` return, and only then added itself to
      `active_workers`; the starter could block on what the thread would
      send, and an idle worker find no timer, no socket and nothing active
      in between. The thread is now counted before the status is published
      (`runtime/c/veles_task.c`). In WSL: without the fix `-count=50` failed
      within four runs; with it, 50 of 50 passed. No test forces the window
      deterministically; `TestExecutors` under `-count` is the pin.
- [ ] **D208 (built 2026-10-24, plan C12), what is left.** (1) The report
      for "the program failed, the compiler passed" is built but not pinned by a
      test (there is no hook to make the runtime wrong). (2) A test that uses
      `try` does not run in the compiler (a `const fun` cannot throw yet, D113),
      nor does a test under a suite's `skip`/`skipIf` gate. (3) Each test has
      the whole constant step budget; a suite of slow tests the compiler can
      run makes `veles test` slower by that much — not measured. (4) Helper
      call sites inside a failing compile-time run are not listed under the
      failure as the runtime lists them.
- [ ] **D199 (built 2026-10-24, plan S17), what is left.** (1) The gate
      modes that print a file's text (`--lines`, `--render`, `--quote`) skip a
      file that is not UTF-8: a Veles string cannot hold its bytes. (2) The
      language server analyses the disk's bytes only while the editor's buffer
      is that file with U+FFFD for each undecodable byte; an editor that
      replaces a run of bad bytes with one U+FFFD (a truncated sequence) can
      put a later column on that line off by one. (3) `benchParse` still reads
      with `fs.readFile` (std is UTF-8). (4) macOS was not run.
- [ ] **B22 left open (D166 built 2026-10-23).** (1) **Labels in a generic
      type's arguments are refused, not kept** (`Box<(lo: i64, hi: i64)>` is an
      error naming `Box<(i64, i64)>`): one struct or sealed instance serves every
      labelling and holds none, because codegen, derived operations and several
      checker tables key instances by identity. Keeping them needs instances per
      labelling that are `Identical` to each other and emitted once by `Key`.
      Built-in collections (`List`, `Map`, …), function results and a throwing
      call's success type keep labels; a `Result` value that is not a direct call
      (a `Result<(a: …), E>` local, `when (r) { is Ok => … }`) shows its payload
      unlabelled. The spec says labels do not change the generic instance; it
      does not say whether a written label may be refused — the user's to
      confirm or lift. (2) A `loop (e in m)` over a map, and `loop ((k, v) in m)`'s
      element, stay `(K, V)`: D166 labels `entries()` only. (3) `[(1, 2): x => x]`
      — a tuple key whose value is a one-parameter lambda without parentheses —
      still reads as a lambda `(1, 2): x => …` (the return-type scan sees `x =>`);
      write `(x) => x`. (4) Not done: rename/references of a label (the label is
      not a declaration the index knows), hover on the label in `t.key` (not
      built or checked), and moving the selfhost's const tables (`range.1 < r`) to labels.
      Windows and Linux (WSL) green.
- [ ] **B21 left open (D163–D165 built 2026-10-23).** One choice that is the
      user's: D163's migration of the tree's `throw X(message: …)` sites "by
      `--fix`, as the user chooses when built" — it needs a lint that offers the
      positional form on a one-parameter construction (a style rule: which
      spelling to prefer, on by default or not); nothing was migrated. (`copy`
      on a type whose `init` takes parameters and on an `extern union`: decided
      2026-10-23, refused — D164 addendum v0.105.)
- **Found by the direction review (2026-10-14, `archive/veles-direction-2026-10-14.md`), open:**
  ~~`implement Codable` on a struct with a tuple field fails with "no method 'encode'"~~
  (fixed with B22, 2026-10-23: the derive names the field); ~~no debug info~~ (S8 built 2026-10-23: gdb steps Veles source; what is left above); no per-target
  code in Veles, every OS difference is in `runtime/c` (D160); `std/net` has no UDP,
  multicast, Unix-domain sockets or lookup API, and there is no file watching, memory-mapped
  file, terminal control, serial, HID, USB or Bluetooth module (D161, plan track P); the
  self-hosted parser is 1.5–1.7× slower than Go's on std after S7's first pass (2026-10-23;
  was 3.0× by `bench/parse`, D198, plan S16, closes the rest); `veles fmt` spreads a
  one-call `unsafe { f() }` over three lines (73 sites in std; plan B18).
- **D168/D169 (decided 2026-10-15/16, built 2026-10-16, plan B23/B24); left out:** a
  first-_success_ race (errors ignored unless every contestant fails — the user: "`race try`
  or something better named"); `race(on: e)` / `race(workers: n)`; `awaitAll(…)` as a
  `select` arm (from D156). Built without: placing a `select`'s or `race`'s tasks on the
  executor of an enclosing `scope(on: e)` (they run where the construct runs); a `race`
  body with a lambda statement at its top level (`val f = x => x`) is read as the removed
  arm form (the parser tells the forms apart by a top-level `=>`); the D35 capture error
  inside `async { }` says "this lambda"; an `async { }` that captures nothing it needs is
  not linted.
- **Found by the idioms pass (2026-10-14, `veles-idioms-and-apis.md` §2.1), open, decision-free:**
  F1 `val Point(x, y) = p` on a plain struct (not a sealed variant) demands `else` though it
  cannot fail; F2 `T: Equatable` / `T: Hashable` bounds refuse `i64`, `string` and structs
  while `T: Comparable` accepts them and `==`/map keys need no bound — at least the error
  should say no bound is needed (making the bounds hold is idea I6, a decision); F3
  `manager: *User? = null` suggests the type `'*User??'` — it should name `(*User)?`; F4
  `list.count()` with no predicate reports "cannot infer type parameter 'E'" instead of
  pointing at `len()`. The ideas I1–I14 and A1–A83 in that file are undecided; a picked one
  goes through `veles-decide`.
- **D156, left out of the decision (2026-10-13):** `awaitAll(…)` as a `race` arm; an unordered
  lazy `mapConcurrent` (results as each finishes).
- **D156, not built yet (F19, 2026-10-13):** the compile-time deadlock warning for `workers:` (a
  loop of launches over more than a constant limit, then a send the launched function waits
  for) — the runtime's deadlock report is all there is; quick fixes for the immediate-await
  warning (two edits: drop the `val`, put the call where the `await` was) and for a placed scope
  that starts nothing (`e.run(() => …)`); the adapters (`map`, `filter`, …) on `AsyncIterator`
  (D42/D46 promise them); a non-Sendable element type given to the lazy `mapConcurrent` is
  reported inside the prelude, not at the call; the lazy form keeps up to `workers` results
  waiting in a channel besides the calls under way (memory `2 × workers`); LSP tests for the
  hovers of a `val` task and of `awaitAll`.

- **Executors (D143, built 2026-10-10), not done yet:** on Windows a thread is kept to CPUs of one
  processor group (a list across groups throws `ThreadError`: CPU sets reach across, but only as a
  preference; groups since 2026-10-10, F11);
  macOS (plan A7) names threads but refuses any priority or CPU list; timers and
  sockets are served by the default pool (when its
  every thread runs a long plain loop the monitor fires a timer within ~1 ms and looks at the
  sockets every 10 ms, 2026-10-10 — not at the reactor's own latency);
  with CPU work on a pool the default pool's request latency
  keeps its median but its tail grows — p99 +20–40 % on Windows, +35–100 % on Linux (WSL), the
  worst request 5–6 ms against ~1.4 ms idle (not explained: SMT siblings and turbo clocks shared with
  the spinning threads are the likely cause; the monitor's look at the pools under the runtime
  lock was the other suspect, but it holds the lock for one pass over the pools' threads every
  5 ms — microseconds, not the milliseconds of the tail, 2026-10-11).

- **Found by the concurrency review (2026-10-09, `archive/veles-concurrency-review.md`), not fixed yet**
  (B1–B6 and the child start latency are fixed — F1–F12): (B4) `bench/idle` measures 249 bytes per
  parked task since F12 (2026-10-11; 345 before; Go 8.7 KB per goroutine), the review's 250 met by
  one byte: the task 160 (its slot), the smallest frame 80, the benchmark's own handle 8. Lower
  needs the root frame inside the task's allocation (a header and a size class's slack) or a
  smaller frame; a debug build also gives every task that calls a rare part (its call chain). A
  task parked inside calls keeps their frames: ~4 KB for a ten-deep
  chain in `std/http`, part of it the tails of 2 KB arena chunks a frame did not fit in.
  Many tasks parking on one channel at once: 100k parked and woken cost 0.08 s of CPU on one
  thread and 1.5 s on 32 (the channel's lock found taken one time in three, the shared run queue)
  — wall 0.08–0.13 s against Go's 0.37 s; a waiter queue without the lock would remove most of it.
  (2026-10-11, Windows, `bench/idle`: 62–67 ms on 1 or 4 threads, 99–116 ms on 32. Ruled out: a
  woken receiver taking the channel's lock only to see its receive done — a lock-free retry
  changed nothing, and was taken out; the wakes, which already skip while a thread spins or
  another wake is on its way, as Go's do. What is left needs a profiler on Linux.) The root
  frame inside the task's allocation would save one header and a size class's slack, at most 8
  bytes a parked task, under a target already met: not pursued unless a profile asks.
  `driver/TestStdHttpUnitTests` failed once in a full WSL run on 2026-10-10 (and timed out once on
  2026-10-09, before F9); it passed in every full run since, 4/4 alone under load, and 60/60 on
  2026-10-11 (`veles test std/http` in a loop on Linux beside `go test ./sema` in a loop, the
  failing output kept — none failed). The two hangs seen on Windows on 2026-10-11 were two
  scheduler bugs, both fixed (D145's notes): a task woken while it ran was not told to look at its
  loops (`TestLoopsAreCancellationPoints` once took 48 s), and a cancellation put off past a
  completed channel operation let the next wait park unseen (std/http's accept loop, caught by
  `VELES_WATCHDOG` in a soak: the 10-minute timeout). A hang that comes back is hunted the same
  way: the driver test binary in a loop under load with `VELES_WATCHDOG=45` (the `veles-debug`
  skill). If `TestStdHttpUnitTests` fails again, its loop is in the plan's progress log of that day.

- **`withTimeout` (D151, built 2026-10-11):** no task per call any more (76 ns, was ~450; `f`'s
  `Result` lands in `withTimeout`'s frame); what a call still allocates is the caller's closure
  for `f` — inlining `withTimeout` at its call site would take it out too. Deadlines on socket operations (Go's) are not
  offered (rejected in D151 unless a profile asks).

- **The collector (F18, built 2026-10-13), not done yet:** the sweep is out of the pause everywhere,
  and on Windows the marking too (bench/gcpause: the loop's longest stall ~1 ms against Go's
  9–35 ms, 1.1–1.2× Go's time). Left:
  (1) **Linux marks in the pause** (and macOS): soft-dirty bits reset for the whole process, so an
  allocating program faults at every page it fills (35 % slower, measured with write watch reset
  everywhere); they are used only when asked (`VELES_GC_CONCURRENT=1`, after a start-up
  self-test) and are not tested here (WSL2's kernel has none). The way to concurrent marking there:
  spans of pointer-free objects apart from the rest (Go's noscan spans) — the kernel writes into
  byte buffers only — and `mprotect` on the quiet spans of the rest, a fault handler recording the
  page written (it must chain with the stack-overflow handler, D92).
  (2) **Live data reachable only from a stack while marking runs** is traced in the last pause: a
  program building a large structure right then pays for it (gcpause's build phase: 4–6 ms).
  (3) **The first collection** of a program marks in one pause (the live size is not known yet:
  3–4 ms for 8 MB).
  (4) **A thread allocating past half again the goal while the helpers mark waits** for them
  (gcpause's build phase: twice, 6 ms each) — it could mark instead (Go's assists).
  (5) Pages read as written at the last pause include the hot spans' (an allocator's), skipped
  span by span when nothing in them is marked; a program whose fresh objects mostly live would
  rescan them all there — rounds that reset only those pages run while the program does.

- **Declared executors (D150, built 2026-10-11), kept for later:** they run until the program ends
  rather than being closed after `main` (nothing of theirs is left running then: their tasks were
  children of scopes); a trait implementation cannot be one of its members. (An executor's
  function taken as a value outside its code stays an error by decision: Q31, 2026-10-11.)

- Visibility (D142, built 2026-10-17: plan B17). Not done: the migration did not
  insert `internal` for the old module-private items (the unwritten level widened
  from the module to the package; a module-only helper is written `internal` when
  wanted), and std keeps its `public`s (some may now be demotable to the package
  level — an API review, not a rule). The spec's "A `public` signature" check
  covers functions and fields, not type aliases, trait methods, `static val`s or
  an `executor`'s functions. A `veles check --fix` over a path inside a populated
  directory fixes its sibling files too (found 2026-10-17). Kept for later:
  a file-scoped `private` on top-level declarations (Kotlin's rule) — refused
  as an error today so the word stays free; take it up if large modules ask
  for per-file helpers. A semver check at `veles publish` that names a field
  without a default added to a `public` type (it breaks other packages'
  construction calls) — not built.
- Arrays (D121): `ref(i)` and in-place `sort`/`swap` are not offered (`set` and
  `loop (&x in a)` are); a type argument may not calculate from a constant
  parameter (`Array<u8, N + 1>`), and a constant parameter is an `i64`; every
  read-only `List` method except `map`/`filter`/`forEach`/`fold`/`any`/`all`/
  `find` copies the array into a list per call; a value of 128 bytes or more
  that holds an array is copied wherever it is passed or assigned (share one
  with a pointer); a local array is on the stack, so one of hundreds of
  megabytes may overflow it (the type allows a gigabyte); a pointer array's
  collector descriptor lists every element's pointer, so `Array<string, N>`
  with a huge `N` has a large descriptor.
- A `with` resource may not leave its block (D100 part 3), but the check is
  local: it sees the value returned, assigned, stored in a collection or
  captured, directly or through a `val` alias or a literal around it — not
  what a function it is lent to does with it (by the decision), nor a value
  derived from it by a call (`conn.reader()`).
- A task handle may not leave its scope (D141) by the same local check: a
  function the handle is passed to may keep it (in a global, say). Awaiting
  it after its scope then panics at run time ("awaited task was cancelled",
  or "awaited task failed; its error left with its scope" when the scope
  failed). An `await` on a failed child inside its scope yields until the
  scope's failure abandons or cancels the awaiter — a short spin on the run
  queue, not a parked wait.
- Run-time concurrency gotchas, documented in
  `docs/documentation/reference/concurrency.md` (2026-10-08), that the
  compiler could catch — each a decision (`veles-decide`) before building:
  a `race` against a task that never suspends cannot stop it under
  `VELES_THREADS=1`, and reports the timeout only after it finished
  (`withTimeout` no longer has this: D151 stops `f` in its own task); `await t`
  after `t.cancel()` panics unless the task had finished — refused at
  compile time where the cancel comes first on every path (D154,
  2026-10-11), left to the panic where it is on some paths only;
  `send` on a closed channel panics (not statically knowable in general).
  A partial deadlock (some tasks stuck while a timer or socket wait exists
  elsewhere) is not detected; only a whole-program one is.
- A child launched with `async` that _returns_ a `Result` without `throws`
  is not a failing child — in a `scope` (D141), in a `gather` (D155,
  2026-10-11; flattened into the gather's error before) and in a D111
  field, all pinned by `driver/taskhandle_test.go`. A D111 field's type
  cannot tell such a task from one that throws, so a field
  `Task<Result<T, E>>` still makes the receiving function `throws E`.
- A test's output is captured through `io` only: what C code writes itself
  (`printf` through FFI) goes straight to the terminal.
- `expectPanics(body)` runs `body` in a task of its own, so `body` must be
  a sendable function: it cannot capture a `MutableList` of the test's.
- A panic std raises for a caller's misuse points at the caller only for the
  functions marked `@caller_location` (D88: `swap`, `insert`, `removeAt`,
  `chunked`, `windowed`, `step`, `toString(radix:)`, `randomBytes`, `Uuid.of`,
  `random.range`, and since 2026-10-11 the suspending `retry`,
  `mapConcurrent`, `forEachConcurrent`); other std panics (`Hmac.update`
  after finish, the JWT key length check) still report the std line.
  `Semaphore(permits:)` and `time.ticker` are marked.
- `T?.decode(from)` written by hand parses as a safe call on `T`; use a
  generic (`fun decodeIt<T: Decodable>(...)`) or a field. Derived code uses a
  resolved-type receiver and is unaffected.
- `crypto.randomBytes` returns the bytes through a `string`-shaped runtime
  buffer and `.bytes()`, the convention `fs.readBytes` already uses. It
  works (the length, not a NUL, delimits the buffer) but a `List<u8>` out
  parameter would be one copy cheaper.
- A `Secret`'s bytes are wiped (D112), but what `expose()` returns, the
  text a `Secret` was made from, and HMAC's pads are ordinary memory.
- A function value that returns a value holding a task (D111), passed
  through a generic function, is refused at the call that passed it
  (`withTimeout(d, () => serving())` since 2026-10-11, its body keeping no
  variable any more); a generic function of the program's own that keeps
  the value in a variable is also reported there, in its body.
- Conditional suspension (D116) covers first-order `suspends` parameters
  only: one whose own parameters are suspending functions (a callback that
  takes a callback) makes its function suspend whenever called. So does a
  parameter the function keeps — stores, returns, or hands to a function
  that is not itself conditional. Trait methods keep their declared
  effects.
- `T implements X` (D117): each instance checks only the branch it takes,
  so a mistake in a branch no instance takes is not reported until one
  does. `static assert(T implements X, "…")` states the requirement per
  instance (B14).
- Constants (D113): a constant can call only a `const fun`; a constant
  `Map`/`Set` whose key type has its own
  `hash`/`equals` is refused (the compiler lays the table out with the
  structural hash); a sealed variant is not a constant value; a table's
  element is a `when` pattern only through a named `const`
  (`const FIRST = T.at(0)`), patterns being names and literals.
- **Deferred from E7 planning (2026-10-05, D138), to build later:** publishing the members of a workspace (per-package versions
  with prefixed tags `pkg/a/v1.2.0`, optional lockstep; path dependencies turned into version dependencies on publish); a local
  override while developing (Go's `replace`) — decide with publishing; the hosted registry service (the protocol and a reference
  server come first); `package.vs` (D131) and its `package` keyword clash; a transparency log for the registry (D139 chose a signed `.info`); replay protection of a signed `.info`; ed25519 in `std/crypto` for a Veles-written client.
- **Deferred from E7 stage (g), the registry (2026-10-07, D139), to build later:** the hosted registry service itself, with
  registration of `owner/name` → repository, the service-side pull from git, the real listing gates (builds on Windows and
  Linux, tests, API diff) and owner identity — the `registry` package is an in-memory reference only (no persistence, no
  `veles registry serve` command, one lock); a transparency log, replay protection of a signed `.info` (an old one with a
  valid signature can be served again), registry key rotation without editing every manifest, more than one registry
  at once (`[registry]` is one address; a dependency cannot choose); `veles publish` from a git tag's exact bytes (it
  zips the directory, so a package with hidden files hashes differently from its git tag); no `veles unpublish`
  (versions are immutable by design); yank state of a cached registry package is refreshed only for a new resolution,
  `fetch` and `audit`, not on every build; reviews: no revocation of a statement, no expiry, no claim vocabulary beyond the
  word (`reviewed` is the only one policy knows), no `attest` for path packages, `import` of a URL has no pinned
  host, keys are plain files with no passphrase; ed25519 is not yet in `std/crypto` (a Veles-written client needs it);
  `veles audit` does not list which statements it ignored (untrusted key, other bytes).
- **Deferred from E7 stage (f), capabilities (2026-10-07), to build later:** `veles deps` does not show capabilities (only `audit` and `add` do); the capability table is by module purpose and is a judgement (`log` reaches `os` and `otel` inside and counts as none; `config` counts as fs and os) — revisit when std grows, and when a std module can be audited by what it calls; a package that spawns a process only through a C library shows as `native`/`extern`, not `os`; no capability for `random`, `time` or `crypto`; no licence field check (`license` is read, nothing uses it); the analysis is by `use` and syntax, so a dependency cannot be caught reaching a capability it never imports (it can only reach through packages the report also lists); policy is per project, with no user-wide default (`~/.veles/policy.toml`); no machine-readable `audit --json` for CI.
- **Deferred from E7 stage (e), the commands (2026-10-07), to build later:** `veles add` does not check the new name against std
  modules and the package's directories (the loader reports it at the next build); `update` has no `--major`, no dry run
  (`--check`) and does not look at yanked versions; `add` accepts one spec at a time; `vendor` copies every version the
  resolution mentions (a smaller vendor would need the graph stored); `veles deps` prints no capabilities or licences yet
  (stage f); `remove`/`update` do not prune `veles.sum` in a workspace; the manifest editor does not touch
  `[dependencies.name]` tables (it says so); `veles fetch`/`deps`/`vendor` take one package, not a whole workspace at once.
- **Deferred from E7 stage (d), fetching (2026-10-05), to build later:** `[dev-dependencies]` are not resolved;
  the proxy protocol is the archive endpoint only — no version list, no checksum log, no yank (stage g), and
  `VELES_PROXY` is the only registry (no default URL until the service exists); a git repository is fetched whole-tag
  (`--depth 1`), a package inside a repository subdirectory is not supported (publishing workspace members); no
  authentication beyond what git does (SSH keys and credential helpers work, prompts are off); fetches run one after
  another, not in parallel; a resolution walks every version mentioned and downloads each (Go's graph pruning would skip
  some); the cache is never cleaned (`veles cache clean`); a workspace resolves each member on its own, not the
  whole workspace at once, so two members may select different versions of one package; the library warning fires for any
  fetched package, not only one with no `main`; a repository that is also reachable as another URL (https vs ssh) is
  two packages; no mirror/proxy fallback for git sources; the module cache is not safe against two builds fetching at
  once beyond the rename (a lost race is accepted).
- **Deferred from E7 stage (c), workspaces (2026-10-05), to build later:** `veles doc` at a workspace root documents the root
  as one package, not each member (`fmt` and the language server likewise treat a member as its own package, with no view of
  the whole root); `veles new --template workspace`; `veles test` at the root does not merge the members' reports into one
  count; members run one after another (no `--jobs` over members, no dependency order).
- **Deferred from E7 stage (a), the manifest (2026-10-05), to build later:** `veles = "…"` (minimum compiler) is read and
  checked but not enforced — the compiler has no version constant yet (add one with the release process, then refuse an older
  compiler with the manifest's line); `[workspace] members` takes directories only, not patterns such as `libs/*`;
  `[dev-dependencies]` are read but nothing loads them yet (`veles test` and `*.test.vs` in stage b/c); the policy table
  (capabilities, tiers: stage f) is not in the schema yet; `[dependencies]` accept an exact version only — no ranges, by D132's MVS;
  a package `version` that is not `major.minor.patch` (the old fixtures used any string) is now an error.
- **Deferred from E3, the reactor (2026-10-08), to build later:** `writev`/`readv` and receiving into a
  list without the copy (`List<u8>` ↔ socket, §3.1); the Windows backend watches sockets only (an
  `ioWait` on another handle is "ready" at once and its call retried); on Windows the thread waiting
  in the reactor keeps the timers to the millisecond (`GetQueuedCompletionStatusEx` takes ms; Go
  associates a high-resolution timer with the completion port) — a parked timekeeper waits on a
  high-resolution waitable timer, ~0.5 ms (F11, 2026-10-10; Linux to tens of µs); timers are 64
  heaps chosen by the task's address, not one per worker as Go's are (a worker that arms and fires
  its own would keep them in its cache). (Idle threads park each on its own since F11 — a futex, an
  event — and are woken by name without the runtime lock.) (Socket waits, the timers and
  the default pool's housekeeping between batches take no runtime lock since F10, 2026-10-10:
  `bench/httphello` at 32 threads now takes as long as at 8 — 178–192 ms on Windows, 0.28 →
  0.34–0.39 s on Linux, where Go's reference goes 0.20 → 0.62 s.)
- **Deferred from `const fun` (2026-10-05), to build later:** `throws` and
  `try`/`catch` inside a `const fun` (a thrown error would be a compile error
  at the constant; today the function is refused); trait objects (a call through
  one is refused; lambdas, closures over `var`s and named `const fun`s as values
  work since 2026-10-08, with `map`/`filter`/`fold`/`sortedBy`… marked); the functions the compiler derives
  for a user type (`equals`, `hash`, `toString`, `compareTo`, the codec) are not
  `const fun`s, so `==` on a struct with a user `equals` and a key with its own
  `hash` in a constant `Map` are refused, though structural `==` and
  interpolation work; marked since 2026-10-08 (and listed in
  `std/testdata/const-functions.txt`): the function-free `List`/`MutableList` and
  `Range` helpers, `Duration`, the `hex`/`base64` encoders and `std/utf8` — still
  not: the lazy iterators (`iter()`, their adapters), the `Result` helpers
  (`Ok`/`Err` and other sealed variants are not evaluated as values), the decoders
  (they throw), `Duration.zero` (a `static val`), the number formatting
  (`toFixed`), `toF64()` (it calls `strtod`) and the other std modules; the float functions
  of the C library (`sin`, `cos`, `tan`, `exp`, `log`, `log2`, `log10`, `pow`,
  `atan2`, `hypot`) are not evaluated because two libraries differ in the last
  bit — a correctly rounded software implementation in the evaluator (or a
  decision to let them differ) would lift it; generic `const fun` bodies are
  validated per instance, as every generic body is (§11 above); a `const fun`
  body that reads a constant calling the same function reports the
  initialization cycle, not a `const fun` cycle; evaluation speed is about
  6 million steps a second (a tree walk over the HIR with a map per call frame),
  enough for tables of thousands of entries — a compiled evaluator or slots
  instead of maps would matter for a self-hosted lexer's Unicode tables;
  `tryConst`, which reads a constant index or a range bound while a body is
  being checked, runs no function (so `TABLE.at(f())` is not read at compile
  time); a long failure chain keeps its five outermost and innermost calls
  only.
- An initialization cycle is found through direct calls and lambdas; a
  call through a trait object or a function value read from elsewhere is
  not followed.
- The built-in types print, compare and hash without the prelude traits,
  so `i64 implements Display` is false although `"$n"` prints it.
- Hashes are plain Veles. They allocate nothing per block, but expect a
  multiple of a hand-tuned C implementation's time; there is no benchmark
  yet to say which multiple (§3.3).
- `time.now()` is the only clock `uuidV7` has, so two processes on one host
  can produce the same (millisecond, counter) pair. Uniqueness comes from
  the 62 random bits, as in v4; only the ordering is per-process.
- `Offset.local(at:)` goes through the C library's `localtime_r`, so it
  reads the host's zone, honours `TZ`, and is as correct as the platform's
  tz data. A fixed offset still cannot answer a _local_ time on a DST
  boundary; that needs the IANA database (§5.6).
- `Timestamp` outside the years 0000–9999 prints ISO 8601's expanded year
  (`+011476-08-15T05:20:00Z`). That text is not RFC 3339, and the parser
  accepts it only so that `parse(t.toString())` reads back; a peer that is
  strict about RFC 3339 will refuse it.
- The last second at each end of the microsecond range prints and does not
  parse back: the parser stops where `secs * 1000000 + micros` would leave
  the i64, and recovering those two seconds needs arithmetic split across
  the boundary that no reader could check. Everything between them round
  trips (`examples/features/time` asserts both ends print).
- `Offset.local(at:)` outside what the host's C library will convert — a
  negative `time_t` or past the year 3000 on the Microsoft CRT — is the
  offset for the _same date in a year the host can do_, not the offset that
  zone actually had then. Without a tz database "the rules did not change"
  is the only assumption available; with one (§5.6) this stops being an
  approximation.
- `Duration.nanos(n).toString()` for the single most negative `n` prints one
  nanosecond short, because `abs()` saturates there rather than overflowing.
  It is the one `Duration` whose text does not read back, and it cannot be
  written as a literal.
- **Deferred from C2/C3/C5/C6 and the test-file rule (2026-10-04), to build later:**
  - `std/compress`: `inflate` could write into a sized buffer (the safepoint poll in each inner loop and
    the oversized-shift selects show in the IR); levels 7–9 on large noisy inputs are slow (no zlib-style
    give-up on a long chain); a Brotli codec, so `http.files` could offer `.br` siblings; `bench/results.md`
    holds two separately recorded gzip/gunzip tables.
  - `tls.Conn` implements `io.Stream` with E1 (TLS), so `http` serves HTTPS through the same code.
  - Codegen: dead-function elimination (every module's unused functions still reach the IR).
  - Unused-import lint: a file with a generic never instantiated skips the warning (its body is not
    checked); scan the generic's body syntactically instead.
  - `std/config`: a `Map` or a list of structs has no variable form and panics at the first call — make it a
    compile-time error; `os.setEnv` (tests give a lookup to the private `loadWith`); `.env` search upward from
    the working directory; `${VAR}` interpolation in dotenv (decided against for now); a JSON file's keys are
    the declared field names, `@key(json: …)` is not consulted; a hand-written `decode` has an opaque schema,
    so such a type is one variable; a generic sealed trait has no derive.
  - `std/fs`: byte-range and shared (read) locks; a copy that keeps permissions and times, and
    an atomic `copy`; on Windows `writeAtomic` cannot replace a file another handle has open (POSIX
    disposition semantics would); `fs.lines` yields `IoError` for a too-long line (an `io.TooLong` would need
    a union as a `Result` error type).
  - Endian bytes: `isize`/`usize` are fixed at 8 bytes (a 32-bit target would change it); no `Array`
    writers (`pushU32Be` is on `MutableList<u8>` only).
  - Tests: **exporting test code from a package** so people can write reusable test libraries (the user's
    "later", D78 amendment). (`TestStdFsUnitTests` failed under a full run because two `fs` tests used the same
    scratch file name while tests run in parallel; fixed by naming. A scratch helper that makes the name
    unique per test by construction would keep it from coming back.)
- **Deferred from C8 template literals (2026-10-04), to build later:** ~~`std/db` and its `sql"…"` (plan E2)~~ (built 2026-10-05);
  std tags such as `html"…"` that escapes and `regex"…"` (the literal is the mechanism, the tags are std
  work and public API); embedded-language highlighting inside a tag's string (SQL, HTML) in the editor
  grammar; LSP semantic tokens for the tag (the TextMate grammar colours it, the language server does
  not); a tag with explicit type arguments (`tag<T>"…"` is not parsed); multi-line and raw literals
  (Veles has neither yet) tagged; when the Veles lexer/parser is written (`veles-selfhost-frontend-plan.md`) it
  needs the `TemplateExpr` node and its adjacency rule, and the self-host oracle compares `(template …)` in `Dump`.
- **Deferred from E2 `std/db` (2026-10-05), to build later:** other databases (MySQL, SQLite) and the pooling of
  prepared statements — every statement is parsed afresh (an unnamed statement), a named-statement cache per
  connection would save the parse and is the first performance step (`veles-bench` it against a tight query loop);
  streaming a large result row by row (a result is read whole into memory; a `maxRows` ceiling and a cursor
  API are the way); `COPY`, `LISTEN`/`NOTIFY`, cancel requests (a statement past its time is cancelled by
  dropping the connection); a retry helper for `isRetryable()` transactions; reading `interval`, `numeric` with a
  fraction, `json`/`jsonb` and multi-dimensional arrays into typed fields (they read as text); `Duration`
  decoding (the decoder's duration style is seconds, the server's text is `HH:MM:SS`); SASLprep of passwords with
  non-ASCII characters (the password is used as written, which only differs for characters that normalise);
  unix-socket and `PGPASSFILE`/`PG*` environment connections; `target_session_attrs` and several hosts;
  `Pool.close()` only closes idle connections (a `Tx` left open now sends `ROLLBACK` at its close, D147, 2026-10-10); `examples/pgnotes` and a `veles new --template` with a database need a server in the example
  harness; `driver/db_test.go` skips when no PostgreSQL binaries are given (`VELES_PG_BIN`), so a machine without them
  runs only the tests that need no server; on Windows PostgreSQL sometimes resets the connection after refusing a login,
  which then shows as `ErrorKind.Connection` rather than `Auth` (the reset discards the unread error); `needsRehash`
  for stored password hashes whose parameters fell behind; `Uuid` is now `Codable` (text form) — a std addition made for
  `std/db`.
- **Deferred from E1 TLS (2026-10-05):** the server span's `url.scheme` is `http` even under `serve(tls:)` (a `Request` does not know it came over TLS); a macOS system TLS backend
  (Homebrew OpenSSL 3 validated with A7, 2026-10-21); client certificates (mutual TLS) and the server asking for
  them; revocation checking (CRL/OCSP) and OCSP stapling; session resumption tickets kept across connections;
  SNI-based certificate choice on one listener; custom cipher or version options (TLS 1.2 is the floor, the
  system picks the rest); a distinct `IoKind` for certificate failures (they are `Other`, `detail` starts `tls:`);
  `readLine`/`readExact` buffering is now copied in `net.Conn`, `fs.File` and `tls.Conn` (a shared `io` helper
  would remove it); `close()` sends `close_notify` without waiting only over a `net.Conn` (D147 addendum: over
  another `io.Stream` it is not sent; `shutdownWrite()` sends it and waits); a stored-key leak on Windows only
  if the process is killed (swept by the next TLS server); the encrypted PEM key; ALPN-driven HTTP/2.
- **Deferred from C7 the HTTP client (2026-10-04), to build later:** `https://` (done 2026-10-05) and `HTTPS_PROXY`;
  `HTTP_PROXY` / `NO_PROXY` and the `proxy:` option of D127 (an absolute-form request to the proxy; `CONNECT`
  for https); transparent `Accept-Encoding: gzip` + decoding (today a compressed answer's bytes are returned
  as they came; `std/compress` has the decoder, a streaming `GzipReader` exists); per-phase limits (connect,
  headers, between body reads) beside the one total `timeout:`; a streaming request body (`Payload` is in
  memory; an upload from a file needs a chunked writer); `Expect: 100-continue` on large uploads; a response
  that is never read or closed leaks its socket until exit (a finalizer, or `Closeable` enforcement beyond
  the D115 warning); a background reaper for idle connections (they are only dropped when the pool is next
  asked); HTTP/2; cookie jar and `Retry-After`; the client span and `traceparent` header (D126, with
  `std/otel`); charsets other than UTF-8 in `text()`; a public `http.Url` type (the parser is private); a
  `redirect` callback; `retry:` on a POST with an idempotency key.
- **Deferred from C4 health endpoints (2026-10-04), to build later:** the readiness `503` during a stop has
  almost no window today — `serve` stops reading requests on its connections as soon as the stop begins, so
  a probe sees a closed or refused connection rather than the 503; a delay between flipping readiness and
  closing (Kubernetes' `preStop` shape, e.g. `drainDelay:`) would make it observable, and is a design choice
  for the user; no `/startupz`; checks cannot report `degraded` (a pass/fail pair only); no spans exclusion
  yet because `std/otel` (D126) is not built — it must skip `quiet` responses; check results are not cached,
  so a probe every second runs every check every second (a `cacheFor:` option).
- **Deferred from `std/otel` (2026-10-05), to build later:** runtime metrics (GC pauses and heap need E5's counters;
  tasks, threads, open connections need runtime accessors) registered automatically as D126 says; a span per
  database query and `db.system` attributes (with `std/db`, E2); `https` export (E1) and the `https` client span
  attributes (`with otel.start(...)` flushes at its close since D147, 2026-10-10; `GzipWriter` and `Pool` could
  use a suspending close too); exponential histograms, exemplars, span
  links, `tracestate`, baggage and the W3C `baggage` header; limits on attributes per span and events per span
  (OTel's defaults are 128); delta temporality; `Retry-After` honoured by the exporter; the span of a retried
  `http.fetch` is one span for all tries (OTel makes one per try); `http.fetch` spans end at the answer's head, not
  when the body is read; HTTP semantic-convention attributes beyond the common ones (`http.request.body.size`,
  `network.protocol.version`, `url.scheme` on the client side); a Prometheus pull endpoint (not decided);
  `Span` is not Sendable (it owns the task-local binding) — a handle is passed instead; id generation is one
  `crypto.randomBytes` call per id (a per-thread buffer would be faster); sampling is by trace-id ratio plus the
  parent's decision only (no rate-limited or tail sampling); the otel module's tests share one process-wide
  pipeline and take turns (a pipeline value instead of a global would let them run at once).
- **Fixed on the way (2026-10-05): a runtime heap overflow in `veles_list_append_list`** (`addAll`, `concat`): the grown
  buffer was allocated in elements instead of bytes, so appending to a list of anything wider than a byte past its
  capacity wrote beyond the buffer and corrupted the next object — seen as a crash in the collector far from the
  append. `std/prelude/list.test.vs` pins it (it fails on the old runtime). Worth an audit: the other runtime list
  and map growth paths were read for the same mistake and have it right; a debug build that checks every slot header
  at each allocation (as the investigation did by hand) would find this class at once — not built.
- **Review of docs and examples (2026-10-18), what it did not cover.** The chapters 4, 6, 7, 9, 10, 15–25 and `reference/concurrency.md` were checked by sweeps and by their tests (every `veles` block compiles and runs), not read line by line. The compiler still carries the removed-syntax diagnostics (`sema/removed.go`, `lint_index.go`, the `self`/`as`/`impl` fixes and their conformance tests) although the language is not public; whether they are deleted is the user's call. Examples still call `padStart(width)` where the width is computed at run time, because a format spec cannot take one yet (chapter 2).
- **User-defined annotations (D174, built 2026-10-18, plan B26): what is left.** ~~`T.members`~~ (built 2026-10-24, D203: `inline loop (m in T.members)` reads names, docs, `Type` and annotations; in a `const fun` a `MemberInfo` has name, doc and index, but `m.has<A>()`/`m.attr<A>()` on a `MemberInfo` value are not built — only on the loop's handle) and `f.set(x, v)` are not built; an annotation `on fun` or `on trait` is checked and kept but nothing reads it yet (it would be reflection over a module's functions); `veles doc` and completion do not list annotations or show their values; hover shows the declaration only; the diagnostics, the AST and the docs still say "attribute" for the compiler's own `@names` and for the family `attributes` (the user's word is "annotation"); the unread warning does not look at generic owners or sealed families; `T.build` skips fields an `init` assigns and respects the visibility of a defaulted field as a constructor does; the Go `Codable` derive (`sema/derive.go`, `derive_print.go`, `derive_schema.go`) and `@key`/`@skip`/`@required`/`@tag` are not yet ordinary annotations in `std/codec` (the point of D174 for the self-hosted compiler); `std/cli` (plan C9) is to be written on it. The reflection answers are compile-time only by design: a function that reads them cannot be called from code that runs, and its emitted body is never reached.
- **Typed decorators (D176, built 2026-10-18): what is left.** Hover on a decorated function does not name the wrapper, and a backtrace shows the lambda frame the rewrite adds; `veles doc` does not mark a decorated function; a decorator on a trait method that has a default is rewritten for the default only (an `implement` that overrides it is not wrapped); the second wrapper shape cannot wrap a function with a variadic parameter; the arguments of an annotation that has a wrapper are checked where the annotation stands, so they cannot name the function's parameters; a bare argument (`@Retry(3)`) needs exactly one parameter until D163 is built, so a decorator with a defaulted second parameter is written `@Retry(times: 3)`.
- **Lint levels and formatter options (D177, parts 1–2 built 2026-10-18, plan B27): what is left.** Part 3 (rules written in Veles: `std/lint`, the reflected AST, the evaluator's speed measured first) is not built. `[lint.forbid]` is not built. Library lints cannot be named yet: the TOML reader has no dotted keys, so they will be written as quoted keys (`"acme.no_sleep" = "warn"`) or move with `package.vs` (D131); the manifest says "not available yet". The warnings now all have registry names (stale-reference, parameter-copy and self-address added 2026-10-18); still outside it are `implicit_return` (an error with its own setting) and the removed-syntax errors in `lint_index.go`, which are not lints. `long_line` counts text blocks (`"""…"""`) and comments like any other line, and a tab as four columns. `trailing_commas` covers parameter lists and broken lists/arguments/literals; a `when` arm's pattern list and `use { }` lists are unaffected (the latter never had one). `line_endings = "auto"` decides per file from its content; a new empty file gets LF.
- **Lint rules written in Veles (D177, plan B27 part 3, built 2026-10-19): what is left.** Built: the evaluator holds sealed variants and `*T` fields while it runs a `const fun` (`CVariant`; a _result_ still cannot be sealed), `std/lint` (`Rule`, `Visitor` with an `on<Node>` callback per `selfhost/ast` struct, `Context.report(span, message, fix:)`, `Fix`; `ast.vs`, `kind.vs`, `source.vs`, `visitor.vs` are generated from `selfhost/` by `go test ./selfhost -run LintAPI -update`), the Go-to-Veles tree reflector and runner (`sema/lintrules.go`, fields matched by name, `ruleFieldAlias` for the spelled-around ones, tested by `TestRuleASTFields`), `[lint] dep.rule = "warn"` (bare dotted, table or quoted keys; bare dotted keys now parse anywhere in `veles.toml`), the dependency's root module loaded for it, `veles check --fix`, `examples/features/lintrules`. Measured: 8,600 lines / 260 KB with three rules (one with five node callbacks) 0.22 s -> 0.29 s; the evaluator itself runs ~2.8 us per node per pass (294k-node probe), mostly allocation (a `big.Int` per integer, a map per call), and was not tuned. Not built: (a) `[lint.forbid]` (TOML shape undecided: `veles-decide`); (b) type-aware rules: a rule sees the parser's tree only; (c) exit callbacks (`Visitor` enters nodes only); (d) `Rule.level` is not read yet and `veles lint --list` does not list a dependency's rules; (e) the package key is the dependency alias, which is an identifier (D177's `acme-lints.` hyphen cannot be written); (f) the checker's own tree nodes (`ResolvedType`, `CheckedExpr`, …) reach a rule as the family's `Bad` node, and a missing one (a Type or Pattern family without a `Bad` node) fails the rule's run with a message; (g) `rules()` runs once per module of the package and the reflection is per file, with no per-file cache and no run in the language server's fast path; (h) rules from git/registry dependencies load through the same resolver but are untested; (i) evaluator speed (`big.Int` -> unboxed small integers, slot-indexed frames) would help every `const fun` and rule sets far bigger than the probe.
- **Security review (2026-10-25): what it left open.** Vulnerability classes were checked against the tree
  (Log4Shell-style lookups, deserialization gadgets, SQL/command injection, memory safety, ReDoS, path traversal,
  request smuggling, decompression bombs, TLS defaults, secrets, Trojan Source, supply chain — all closed by design or
  by earlier fixes, §2). Fixed here: hash flooding and forged log lines (§2). Left:
  (a) ~~Integer overflow wraps silently in release (D21)~~ — decided 2026-10-25: checked in every build (D209).
  (b) **A user `hash()` is not keyed.** The map scrambles whatever it returns, which defeats keys that differ only in
  high bits, but a user hash with full 64-bit collisions (e.g. `x * 31 + y` over chosen `x`, `y`) still floods. A
  keyed `Hasher` passed to `hash` (Rust's shape), or deriving `Hashable` from the fields through the keyed combine,
  would close it — public API, needs a decision.
  (c) **XSS**: `html"…"` that escapes (D129, listed above) is not built; HTML assembled from strings can be injected.
  (d) **SSRF**: the HTTP client has no opt-in refusal of loopback/private/link-local addresses (checked after DNS,
  on every redirect) for servers that fetch URLs a user gives them — public API, needs a decision.
  (e) **Rules for features not built yet**: an XML reader never resolves external entities or fetches a DTD (XXE); any
  std archive unpacker (zip, tar) applies `fetch`'s refusals (`..`, links, `:`/`\`, case-only duplicates, sizes —
  zip slip); every new parser ships with size and depth limits and a fuzz target.
  (f) JSON log lines and `json.encode` leave DEL and C1 (U+007F–U+009F) unescaped — valid JSON, but `cat` of a log
  file sends them to the terminal; escaping them changes `json.encode`'s output.
  (g) The hash key falls back to clock and ASLR addresses when the OS generator fails (never seen); a WebAssembly
  target (D180) needs `__int128` (clang provides it there) or a 32-bit `hash_mum`.
  (h) The security guidance page (§7, `[~]`) should gather these: secrets, TLS defaults, limits, hashing, logging.
  (i) D220 stdin text and byte readers were verified on macOS only (2026-10-29). Run the full suite on Windows and WSL/Linux before claiming platform parity.
